Like it starts with something concrete — an invoice I need to send, or a client message I haven’t answered — and then even when I’m technically done for the day or I’m trying to sit on the couch and relax, my brain keeps circling back to it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m just worrying about everything in the world out of nowhere. It’s more that since I tried to make my art into a real freelance thing, those business parts of it have become this constant pressure. I’ll avoid the message or the invoice because I feel wound up and unsure what to do, and then later I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to relax. I’ll replay it, like, “Why didn’t I just answer that? Now it’s going to be awkward. Now I look irresponsible.” And then I get more upset with myself.\n\nSometimes it goes as far as me sitting at the kitchen table crying over something that should be simple, like a client reply or payment thing. And then I’ll realize I forgot some normal responsibility too, like paying a bill, because I was so tangled up in the work stuff. So yes, it can follow me into downtime, but it’s still usually attached to those specific tasks and the feeling that I’m not handling them the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there isn’t any specific work stress or task to deal with?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I have had moments where I feel down, but when I try to trace it back, it usually seems connected to feeling overwhelmed or like I’m not keeping up with the work-related stuff. But once I’m wound up about them, it kind of spills over, like I’ll be so stuck on the invoice or message that I forget something normal, like paying a bill. Then I get upset because it feels like I’m failing at basic life stuff too. I’ve ended up crying at my kitchen table over it, which feels ridiculous to say out loud, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: When you feel overwhelmed by things like client messages or invoices, do you notice any physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, shaking, or trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’ve really paid close attention to that, honestly. I know I feel tense, like really wound up, and sometimes I’ll just sit there staring at the email or the invoice screen feeling like I can’t make myself start. But in terms of, like, a racing heart or sweating or shaking, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more like everything feels tight and too much in my head, if that makes sense. Like I get stuck thinking, “Okay, I need to answer this correctly,” and then I second-guess every word. I might feel physically uncomfortable, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble breathing or anything like that. I think I’m usually so focused on the task and how badly I’m handling it that I don’t really check what my body is doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worry or anxiety continues even when you’re not thinking about those specific work-related tasks, like when you’re trying to relax or sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but usually because I’ve already gotten stuck on one of those things earlier, if that makes sense. Like it starts with something concrete — an invoice I need to send, or a client message I haven’t answered — and then even when I’m technically done for the day or I’m trying to sit on the couch and relax, my brain keeps circling back to it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m just worrying about everything in the world out of nowhere. It’s more that since I tried to make my art into a real freelance thing, those business parts of it have become this constant pressure. I’ll avoid the message or the invoice because I feel wound up and unsure what to do, and then later I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to relax. I’ll replay it, like, “Why didn’t I just answer that? Now it’s going to be awkward. Now I look irresponsible.” And then I get more upset with myself.\n\nSometimes it goes as far as me sitting at the kitchen table crying over something that should be simple, like a client reply or payment thing. And then I’ll realize I forgot some normal responsibility too, like paying a bill, because I was so tangled up in the work stuff. So yes, it can follow me into downtime, but it’s still usually attached to those specific tasks and the feeling that I’m not handling them the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there isn’t any specific work stress or task to deal with?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I have had moments where I feel down, but when I try to trace it back, it usually seems connected to feeling overwhelmed or like I’m not keeping up with the work-related stuff. I don’t think I’m just sitting there feeling sad for no reason when nothing is going on.\n\nAnd with interest in things… I don’t know. I still care about the things I usually care about, especially my art. It’s just that sometimes the business side of it makes everything feel heavy, so then even the enjoyable parts can feel tangled up with pressure. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed losing interest across the board or feeling sad completely separate from all of that. It’s more confusing than that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when things weren’t stressful at work or with your art business—just a normal, quiet day—where you still felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time thinking of a clear example of that. Like, if it’s really a normal, quiet day and there isn’t some client thing hanging over me or some art-business task I’m avoiding, I don’t know that I’m sitting there feeling hopeless or uninterested in everything.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel kind of tired or flat, but when I look closer, there’s usually something in the background I haven’t dealt with, even if I’m trying not to think about it. Like an email I didn’t answer or something I feel behind on. So it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, this was totally separate.”\n\nI still usually want to do the things I enjoy when they don’t feel connected to pressure. It’s more that the stress sort of bleeds into everything, and then I start questioning myself. But no, I can’t really point to a quiet, unstressful day where I was just down or hopeless for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that way. I definitely get really wound up and uncomfortable when I’m facing certain things, but I wouldn’t describe it as this sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast with all those physical symptoms.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense or upset, and sometimes I’ll notice my body is kind of reacting because I’m stressed, but when you say racing heart, sweating, trembling, can’t breathe — like an episode — I’m not sure I’ve had something like that. It’s more that I build up anxiety around whatever I’m avoiding or worried about, and then I get stuck in it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had sudden intense attacks like that. Not that I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself especially worried about how others perceive you or whether you're coming across as professional and capable, especially in work-related interactions like emails or client conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s a big part of it, I think. Like, I get really caught up in whether I’m sounding professional enough, or if the client thinks I’m disorganized, or if an invoice makes me look too demanding or clueless or something.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. Ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business instead of just… making art, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices and client messages. I’ll rewrite an email a million times, or avoid opening it, because I’m worried I’m going to come across wrong or not capable. And then the longer I avoid it, the worse it gets, because then I actually am late responding, which makes me feel even less professional.\n\nIt sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it can turn into me sitting at my kitchen table crying over something like an invoice or a message I need to send. And then I’m so preoccupied with that that I’ll forget normal life stuff too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one work-related thing and how badly I might be messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work-related tasks and responsibilities, have you noticed that your worry or self-doubt has affected other important areas of your life, like personal relationships, social interactions, or activities you used to participate in comfortably?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, but mostly in the practical, day-to-day way, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not sure I’d say it’s taken over every part of my life, but it’s definitely started spilling outside just the one task I’m worried about.\n\nFor example, with the art display I was trying to keep up with, I got so focused on making sure everything looked right and that I wasn’t disappointing anyone that I kept putting off a client reply. And then that reply became this whole thing in my head, like I’d made it worse by waiting, so then I avoided it even more.\n\nAnd at home, I actually missed a utility payment, which was embarrassing because it’s not like I didn’t know bills exist. It was more like my attention was completely wrapped up in trying to manage the art stuff and the client communication, and other normal responsibilities just kind of fell through the cracks.\n\nSo I guess yes — it’s affecting my freelance work directly, but also home responsibilities too. It’s not just me feeling stressed internally; it’s starting to show up in things I’m not getting done.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worry about being judged or coming across the wrong way in professional interactions affects your ability to engage in other social situations, even those not related to work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it affecting social situations in the same way, or at least not as clearly.\n\nLike, if I’m talking to a friend or I’m just around people casually, I might still be a little self-conscious sometimes, but it doesn’t have that same weight as when it’s tied to work or clients or trying to look like I know what I’m doing professionally. That’s where I get really tangled up in how I’m coming across.\n\nWith non-work social stuff, I don’t think I’m avoiding things because I’m worried about being judged in the same way. Or if it happens, it’s not something I’ve really connected as a major problem. It’s more noticeable when there’s some kind of responsibility or expectation attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Even in casual social situations, like hanging out with friends or meeting up with someone you know well, do you ever find yourself worrying about how you’re coming across or if you’re saying the right thing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I’m sure I have normal moments where I replay something I said and think, “That sounded awkward,” or I wonder if I talked too much or came off weird, but that feels kind of… ordinary? Like it passes pretty quickly.\n\nWith friends or people I know well, I’m usually not sitting there analyzing every sentence the way I do with a client email or something professional. It’s more like I can relax a little because there isn’t that same sense of, “This has to prove I’m competent,” or “If I say this wrong, they’ll think I’m unprofessional.”\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I can be self-conscious sometimes casually, but I wouldn’t say it’s a major issue or something I’ve really noticed interfering with hanging out with people. It’s much more tied to situations where I feel like there’s some responsibility or expectation attached.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting anxious or stressed in advance about upcoming work tasks, like writing an email or preparing an invoice, even before you actually have to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’m not totally sure. I mean, speaking in front of a group isn’t something I do a lot, so it’s hard for me to say how much of a pattern that is. Then the longer I avoid it, the worse it gets, and I end up crying at my kitchen table because I feel like I’m failing at something I’m supposed to be excited about.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling over into regular life too. Like I’ll forget normal things, like paying a bill, because I’m stuck in this loop of stressing about work stuff and then not doing it. I just… I don’t feel like I’m handling it the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or overwhelmed about things that aren’t related to your work or art, like personal relationships or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has started to, even though it kind of still circles back to the art stuff in my head. Like, it’s not just me feeling stressed while I’m painting or trying to set up an art display. It’s that I’ll get so caught up trying to keep all of that together that other basic things start slipping.\n\nFor example, I put off replying to a client because I felt overwhelmed by what to say and when to say it, and then while I was stressing about that and trying to keep up with the display, I completely missed a utility payment. That felt awful, because it’s such a normal adult responsibility, you know? And then I started beating myself up like, if I can’t even answer one message and pay one bill on time, how am I supposed to manage anything?\n\nSo, yes… everyday responsibilities are getting affected. Not constantly every single second, but enough that it’s making me feel like I’m not really keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a variety of things, even when there isn't a clear reason for concern, or does your anxiety usually center around specific situations like work tasks or client interactions?\n\nPatient: It’s more centered around specific things, I think. Like, I don’t wake up worrying about every possible thing for no reason, or at least that’s not how it feels to me. It’s more like there are these particular tasks tied to trying to make my art into an actual freelance business — invoices, client messages, payment stuff, anything where I have to be professional and organized — and those are the things that set me off.\n\nI’ll see an email from a client or know I need to send an invoice, and it’s like my whole body tenses up. Then I start overthinking what to say, or whether I’m doing it wrong, or whether I’m going to seem unprofessional. And then instead of just doing the thing, I avoid it, which makes it build up even more.\n\nSo it’s not random exactly. It’s tied to those situations. But once I’m wound up about them, it kind of spills over, like I’ll be so stuck on the invoice or message that I forget something normal, like paying a bill. Then I get upset because it feels like I’m failing at basic life stuff too. I’ve ended up crying at my kitchen table over it, which feels ridiculous to say out loud, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: When you feel overwhelmed by things like client messages or invoices, do you notice any physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, shaking, or trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’ve really paid close attention to that, honestly. I know I feel tense, like really wound up, and sometimes I’ll just sit there staring at the email or the invoice screen feeling like I can’t make myself start. But in terms of, like, a racing heart or sweating or shaking, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more like everything feels tight and too much in my head, if that makes sense. Like I get stuck thinking, “Okay, I need to answer this correctly,” and then I second-guess every word. I might feel physically uncomfortable, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble breathing or anything like that. I think I’m usually so focused on the task and how badly I’m handling it that I don’t really check what my body is doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worry or anxiety continues even when you’re not thinking about those specific work-related tasks, like when you’re trying to relax or sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but usually because I’ve already gotten stuck on one of those things earlier, if that makes sense. Like it starts with something concrete — an invoice I need to send, or a client message I haven’t answered — and then even when I’m technically done for the day or I’m trying to sit on the couch and relax, my brain keeps circling back to it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m just worrying about everything in the world out of nowhere. It’s more that since I tried to make my art into a real freelance thing, those business parts of it have become this constant pressure. I’ll avoid the message or the invoice because I feel wound up and unsure what to do, and then later I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to relax. I’ll replay it, like, “Why didn’t I just answer that? Now it’s going to be awkward. Now I look irresponsible.” And then I get more upset with myself.\n\nSometimes it goes as far as me sitting at the kitchen table crying over something that should be simple, like a client reply or payment thing. And then I’ll realize I forgot some normal responsibility too, like paying a bill, because I was so tangled up in the work stuff. So yes, it can follow me into downtime, but it’s still usually attached to those specific tasks and the feeling that I’m not handling them the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there isn’t any specific work stress or task to deal with?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I have had moments where I feel down, but when I try to trace it back, it usually seems connected to feeling overwhelmed or like I’m not keeping up with the work-related stuff. I don’t think I’m just sitting there feeling sad for no reason when nothing is going on.\n\nAnd with interest in things… I don’t know. I still care about the things I usually care about, especially my art. It’s just that sometimes the business side of it makes everything feel heavy, so then even the enjoyable parts can feel tangled up with pressure. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed losing interest across the board or feeling sad completely separate from all of that. It’s more confusing than that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when things weren’t stressful at work or with your art business—just a normal, quiet day—where you still felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time thinking of a clear example of that. Like, if it’s really a normal, quiet day and there isn’t some client thing hanging over me or some art-business task I’m avoiding, I don’t know that I’m sitting there feeling hopeless or uninterested in everything.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel kind of tired or flat, but when I look closer, there’s usually something in the background I haven’t dealt with, even if I’m trying not to think about it. Like an email I didn’t answer or something I feel behind on. So it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, this was totally separate.”\n\nI still usually want to do the things I enjoy when they don’t feel connected to pressure. It’s more that the stress sort of bleeds into everything, and then I start questioning myself. But no, I can’t really point to a quiet, unstressful day where I was just down or hopeless for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that way. I definitely get really wound up and uncomfortable when I’m facing certain things, but I wouldn’t describe it as this sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast with all those physical symptoms.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense or upset, and sometimes I’ll notice my body is kind of reacting because I’m stressed, but when you say racing heart, sweating, trembling, can’t breathe — like an episode — I’m not sure I’ve had something like that. It’s more that I build up anxiety around whatever I’m avoiding or worried about, and then I get stuck in it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had sudden intense attacks like that. Not that I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself especially worried about how others perceive you or whether you're coming across as professional and capable, especially in work-related interactions like emails or client conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s a big part of it, I think. Like, I get really caught up in whether I’m sounding professional enough, or if the client thinks I’m disorganized, or if an invoice makes me look too demanding or clueless or something.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. Ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business instead of just… making art, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices and client messages. I’ll rewrite an email a million times, or avoid opening it, because I’m worried I’m going to come across wrong or not capable. And then the longer I avoid it, the worse it gets, because then I actually am late responding, which makes me feel even less professional.\n\nIt sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it can turn into me sitting at my kitchen table crying over something like an invoice or a message I need to send. And then I’m so preoccupied with that that I’ll forget normal life stuff too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one work-related thing and how badly I might be messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work-related tasks and responsibilities, have you noticed that your worry or self-doubt has affected other important areas of your life, like personal relationships, social interactions, or activities you used to participate in comfortably?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, but mostly in the practical, day-to-day way, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not sure I’d say it’s taken over every part of my life, but it’s definitely started spilling outside just the one task I’m worried about.\n\nFor example, with the art display I was trying to keep up with, I got so focused on making sure everything looked right and that I wasn’t disappointing anyone that I kept putting off a client reply. And then that reply became this whole thing in my head, like I’d made it worse by waiting, so then I avoided it even more.\n\nAnd at home, I actually missed a utility payment, which was embarrassing because it’s not like I didn’t know bills exist. It was more like my attention was completely wrapped up in trying to manage the art stuff and the client communication, and other normal responsibilities just kind of fell through the cracks.\n\nSo I guess yes — it’s affecting my freelance work directly, but also home responsibilities too. It’s not just me feeling stressed internally; it’s starting to show up in things I’m not getting done.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worry about being judged or coming across the wrong way in professional interactions affects your ability to engage in other social situations, even those not related to work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it affecting social situations in the same way, or at least not as clearly.\n\nLike, if I’m talking to a friend or I’m just around people casually, I might still be a little self-conscious sometimes, but it doesn’t have that same weight as when it’s tied to work or clients or trying to look like I know what I’m doing professionally. That’s where I get really tangled up in how I’m coming across.\n\nWith non-work social stuff, I don’t think I’m avoiding things because I’m worried about being judged in the same way. Or if it happens, it’s not something I’ve really connected as a major problem. It’s more noticeable when there’s some kind of responsibility or expectation attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Even in casual social situations, like hanging out with friends or meeting up with someone you know well, do you ever find yourself worrying about how you’re coming across or if you’re saying the right thing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I’m sure I have normal moments where I replay something I said and think, “That sounded awkward,” or I wonder if I talked too much or came off weird, but that feels kind of… ordinary? Like it passes pretty quickly.\n\nWith friends or people I know well, I’m usually not sitting there analyzing every sentence the way I do with a client email or something professional. It’s more like I can relax a little because there isn’t that same sense of, “This has to prove I’m competent,” or “If I say this wrong, they’ll think I’m unprofessional.”\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I can be self-conscious sometimes casually, but I wouldn’t say it’s a major issue or something I’ve really noticed interfering with hanging out with people. It’s much more tied to situations where I feel like there’s some responsibility or expectation attached.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting anxious or stressed in advance about upcoming work tasks, like writing an email or preparing an invoice, even before you actually have to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It actually starts before the task most of the time.\n\nLike with an invoice, it’s not just sitting down and making the invoice. I’ll know I need to do it, and then I start thinking about whether I’m charging the right amount, whether it looks professional enough, whether the client is going to think I’m disorganized or inexperienced. And then somehow this tiny administrative thing starts feeling like proof of whether I’m actually capable of doing this freelance art thing at all.\n\nSame with client messages. I’ll see that I need to reply, and even before I open the email fully, I’m already tense. I’ll draft things in my head, second-guess the wording, worry I’m going to sound too pushy or too vague or not professional enough. And then because I’m stressed about doing it wrong, I put it off, which makes me more stressed because now I’ve waited too long.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. Ever since I started trying to make my art into a real freelance business, these normal work tasks have been getting me really wound up. It builds up until I’m avoiding them, and then I end up crying at my kitchen table over something like an invoice or a client message. And meanwhile regular things, like paying a bill, can slip my mind because I’m so wrapped up in the stress of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or tense even when there’s no specific task or responsibility coming up, like when you’re just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… not usually in a free-floating way, if that makes sense. It’s mostly when something has gotten attached to the freelance art stuff, even if I’m not physically doing the task right that second.\n\nLike I might be sitting on the couch trying to relax, but if I know there’s an invoice I haven’t sent or a client message I need to answer, it’s kind of humming in the background. That’s when I get wound up. It’s not like I’m just peacefully doing something I enjoy and anxiety comes out of nowhere for no reason — it’s more that these work responsibilities sort of follow me around in my head.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. Ever since I started trying to turn my art into more of a real freelance business, normal things like invoices or client messages have become these huge stress points. I’ll avoid them because I feel tense and unsure, and then the avoiding makes it worse, until I’m crying at my kitchen table over something that should be simple. Or I get so wrapped up in it that regular everyday stuff, like paying a bill, slips my mind.\n\nSo, yes, I can feel tense while I’m technically “relaxing,” but usually there’s some specific responsibility hanging over me. It doesn’t feel totally random.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in situations where you might be observed or evaluated by others, like speaking in front of a group or even just eating in public?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, speaking in front of a group isn’t something I do a lot, so it’s hard for me to say how much of a pattern that is. I think most people would feel a little nervous if they had to present something, and I probably would too, but I don’t know that it’s a big defining thing for me.\n\nEating in public… no, not really. I don’t usually think about people watching me eat or judging how I’m acting in that kind of setting. If I’m at a restaurant or coffee shop, I’m usually just focused on whoever I’m with or what I’m doing.\n\nI guess if I felt like I was being evaluated, like someone was looking at my work or judging whether I seemed professional, that would feel different. But just being observed in normal public situations doesn’t really stand out as something I worry about much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll know I need to do it, and then I start thinking about whether I’m charging the right amount, whether it looks professional enough, whether the client is going to think I’m disorganized or inexperienced. And then somehow this tiny administrative thing starts feeling like proof of whether I’m actually capable of doing this freelance art thing at all.\n\nSame with client messages. I’ll see that I need to reply, and even before I open the email fully, I’m already tense. I’ll draft things in my head, second-guess the wording, worry I’m going to sound too pushy or too vague or not professional enough. And then because I’m stressed about doing it wrong, I put it off, which makes me more stressed because now I’ve waited too long.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. Ever since I started trying to make my art into a real freelance business, these normal work tasks have been getting me really wound up. It builds up until I’m avoiding them, and then I end up crying at my kitchen table over something like an invoice or a client message. And meanwhile regular things, like paying a bill, can slip my mind because I’m so wrapped up in the stress of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or tense even when there’s no specific task or responsibility coming up, like when you’re just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… not usually in a free-floating way, if that makes sense. It’s mostly when something has gotten attached to the freelance art stuff, even if I’m not physically doing the task right that second.\n\nLike I might be sitting on the couch trying to relax, but if I know there’s an invoice I haven’t sent or a client message I need to answer, it’s kind of humming in the background. That’s when I get wound up. It’s not like I’m just peacefully doing something I enjoy and anxiety comes out of nowhere for no reason — it’s more that these work responsibilities sort of follow me around in my head.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. Ever since I started trying to turn my art into more of a real freelance business, normal things like invoices or client messages have become these huge stress points. I’ll avoid them because I feel tense and unsure, and then the avoiding makes it worse, until I’m crying at my kitchen table over something that should be simple. Or I get so wrapped up in it that regular everyday stuff, like paying a bill, slips my mind.\n\nSo, yes, I can feel tense while I’m technically “relaxing,” but usually there’s some specific responsibility hanging over me. I don’t think so, not really. At least, I haven’t noticed that in a broad way.

Like, if I’m going to a regular event or doing something in a group, I might have the normal kind of “what should I wear, am I going to know people there” thoughts, but it doesn’t usually stop me from going or make me feel like I’m being watched the whole time. It’s that I’ll get so caught up trying to keep all of that together that other basic things start slipping.\n\nFor example, I put off replying to a client because I felt overwhelmed by what to say and when to say it, and then while I was stressing about that and trying to keep up with the display, I completely missed a utility payment. It’s more like there are these particular tasks tied to trying to make my art into an actual freelance business — invoices, client messages, payment stuff, anything where I have to be professional and organized — and those are the things that set me off.\n\nI’ll see an email from a client or know I need to send an invoice, and it’s like my whole body tenses up. But once I’m wound up about them, it kind of spills over, like I’ll be so stuck on the invoice or message that I forget something normal, like paying a bill. Then I get upset because it feels like I’m failing at basic life stuff too. I’ve ended up crying at my kitchen table over it, which feels ridiculous to say out loud, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: When you feel overwhelmed by things like client messages or invoices, do you notice any physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, shaking, or trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’ve really paid close attention to that, honestly. I know I feel tense, like really wound up, and sometimes I’ll just sit there staring at the email or the invoice screen feeling like I can’t make myself start. But in terms of, like, a racing heart or sweating or shaking, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more like everything feels tight and too much in my head, if that makes sense. Like I get stuck thinking, “Okay, I need to answer this correctly,” and then I second-guess every word. I might feel physically uncomfortable, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble breathing or anything like that. I think I’m usually so focused on the task and how badly I’m handling it that I don’t really check what my body is doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worry or anxiety continues even when you’re not thinking about those specific work-related tasks, like when you’re trying to relax or sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but usually because I’ve already gotten stuck on one of those things earlier, if that makes sense. Like it starts with something concrete — an invoice I need to send, or a client message I haven’t answered — and then even when I’m technically done for the day or I’m trying to sit on the couch and relax, my brain keeps circling back to it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m just worrying about everything in the world out of nowhere. It’s more that since I tried to make my art into a real freelance thing, those business parts of it have become this constant pressure. I’ll avoid the message or the invoice because I feel wound up and unsure what to do, and then later I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to relax. I’ll replay it, like, “Why didn’t I just answer that? Now it’s going to be awkward. Now I look irresponsible.” And then I get more upset with myself.\n\nSometimes it goes as far as me sitting at the kitchen table crying over something that should be simple, like a client reply or payment thing. And then I’ll realize I forgot some normal responsibility too, like paying a bill, because I was so tangled up in the work stuff. So yes, it can follow me into downtime, but it’s still usually attached to those specific tasks and the feeling that I’m not handling them the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there isn’t any specific work stress or task to deal with?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, I have had moments where I feel down, but when I try to trace it back, it usually seems connected to feeling overwhelmed or like I’m not keeping up with the work-related stuff. I don’t think I’m just sitting there feeling sad for no reason when nothing is going on.\n\nAnd with interest in things… I don’t know. I still care about the things I usually care about, especially my art. It’s just that sometimes the business side of it makes everything feel heavy, so then even the enjoyable parts can feel tangled up with pressure. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed losing interest across the board or feeling sad completely separate from all of that. It’s more confusing than that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when things weren’t stressful at work or with your art business—just a normal, quiet day—where you still felt down, hopeless, or uninterested in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m having a hard time thinking of a clear example of that. Like, if it’s really a normal, quiet day and there isn’t some client thing hanging over me or some art-business task I’m avoiding, I don’t know that I’m sitting there feeling hopeless or uninterested in everything.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel kind of tired or flat, but when I look closer, there’s usually something in the background I haven’t dealt with, even if I’m trying not to think about it. Like an email I didn’t answer or something I feel behind on. So it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, this was totally separate.”\n\nI still usually want to do the things I enjoy when they don’t feel connected to pressure. It’s more that the stress sort of bleeds into everything, and then I start questioning myself. But no, I can’t really point to a quiet, unstressful day where I was just down or hopeless for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that came on very quickly and included physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, trembling, or feeling like you couldn’t breathe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in that way. I definitely get really wound up and uncomfortable when I’m facing certain things, but I wouldn’t describe it as this sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast with all those physical symptoms.\n\nI mean, I can feel tense or upset, and sometimes I’ll notice my body is kind of reacting because I’m stressed, but when you say racing heart, sweating, trembling, can’t breathe — like an episode — I’m not sure I’ve had something like that. It’s more that I build up anxiety around whatever I’m avoiding or worried about, and then I get stuck in it.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had sudden intense attacks like that. Not that I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself especially worried about how others perceive you or whether you're coming across as professional and capable, especially in work-related interactions like emails or client conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s a big part of it, I think. Like, I get really caught up in whether I’m sounding professional enough, or if the client thinks I’m disorganized, or if an invoice makes me look too demanding or clueless or something.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. Ever since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business instead of just… making art, I keep getting so wound up over things like invoices and client messages. I’ll rewrite an email a million times, or avoid opening it, because I’m worried I’m going to come across wrong or not capable. And then the longer I avoid it, the worse it gets, because then I actually am late responding, which makes me feel even less professional.\n\nIt sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it can turn into me sitting at my kitchen table crying over something like an invoice or a message I need to send. And then I’m so preoccupied with that that I’ll forget normal life stuff too, like paying a bill, because my brain is just stuck on this one work-related thing and how badly I might be messing it up.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work-related tasks and responsibilities, have you noticed that your worry or self-doubt has affected other important areas of your life, like personal relationships, social interactions, or activities you used to participate in comfortably?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, but mostly in the practical, day-to-day way, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not sure I’d say it’s taken over every part of my life, but it’s definitely started spilling outside just the one task I’m worried about.\n\nFor example, with the art display I was trying to keep up with, I got so focused on making sure everything looked right and that I wasn’t disappointing anyone that I kept putting off a client reply. And then that reply became this whole thing in my head, like I’d made it worse by waiting, so then I avoided it even more.\n\nAnd at home, I actually missed a utility payment, which was embarrassing because it’s not like I didn’t know bills exist. It was more like my attention was completely wrapped up in trying to manage the art stuff and the client communication, and other normal responsibilities just kind of fell through the cracks.\n\nSo I guess yes — it’s affecting my freelance work directly, but also home responsibilities too. It’s not just me feeling stressed internally; it’s starting to show up in things I’m not getting done.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worry about being judged or coming across the wrong way in professional interactions affects your ability to engage in other social situations, even those not related to work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it affecting social situations in the same way, or at least not as clearly.\n\nLike, if I’m talking to a friend or I’m just around people casually, I might still be a little self-conscious sometimes, but it doesn’t have that same weight as when it’s tied to work or clients or trying to look like I know what I’m doing professionally. That’s where I get really tangled up in how I’m coming across.\n\nWith non-work social stuff, I don’t think I’m avoiding things because I’m worried about being judged in the same way. Or if it happens, it’s not something I’ve really connected as a major problem. It’s more noticeable when there’s some kind of responsibility or expectation attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Even in casual social situations, like hanging out with friends or meeting up with someone you know well, do you ever find yourself worrying about how you’re coming across or if you’re saying the right thing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I’m sure I have normal moments where I replay something I said and think, “That sounded awkward,” or I wonder if I talked too much or came off weird, but that feels kind of… ordinary? Like it passes pretty quickly.\n\nWith friends or people I know well, I’m usually not sitting there analyzing every sentence the way I do with a client email or something professional. It’s more like I can relax a little because there isn’t that same sense of, “This has to prove I’m competent,” or “If I say this wrong, they’ll think I’m unprofessional.”\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I can be self-conscious sometimes casually, but I wouldn’t say it’s a major issue or something I’ve really noticed interfering with hanging out with people. It’s much more tied to situations where I feel like there’s some responsibility or expectation attached.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting anxious or stressed in advance about upcoming work tasks, like writing an email or preparing an invoice, even before you actually have to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It actually starts before the task most of the time.\n\nLike with an invoice, it’s not just sitting down and making the invoice. I’ll know I need to do it, and then I start thinking about whether I’m charging the right amount, whether it looks professional enough, whether the client is going to think I’m disorganized or inexperienced. And then somehow this tiny administrative thing starts feeling like proof of whether I’m actually capable of doing this freelance art thing at all.\n\nSame with client messages. I’ll see that I need to reply, and even before I open the email fully, I’m already tense. I’ll draft things in my head, second-guess the wording, worry I’m going to sound too pushy or too vague or not professional enough. And then because I’m stressed about doing it wrong, I put it off, which makes me more stressed because now I’ve waited too long.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. Ever since I started trying to make my art into a real freelance business, these normal work tasks have been getting me really wound up. It builds up until I’m avoiding them, and then I end up crying at my kitchen table over something like an invoice or a client message. And meanwhile regular things, like paying a bill, can slip my mind because I’m so wrapped up in the stress of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or tense even when there’s no specific task or responsibility coming up, like when you’re just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… not usually in a free-floating way, if that makes sense. It’s mostly when something has gotten attached to the freelance art stuff, even if I’m not physically doing the task right that second.\n\nLike I might be sitting on the couch trying to relax, but if I know there’s an invoice I haven’t sent or a client message I need to answer, it’s kind of humming in the background. That’s when I get wound up. It’s not like I’m just peacefully doing something I enjoy and anxiety comes out of nowhere for no reason — it’s more that these work responsibilities sort of follow me around in my head.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. Ever since I started trying to turn my art into more of a real freelance business, normal things like invoices or client messages have become these huge stress points. I’ll avoid them because I feel tense and unsure, and then the avoiding makes it worse, until I’m crying at my kitchen table over something that should be simple. Or I get so wrapped up in it that regular everyday stuff, like paying a bill, slips my mind.\n\nSo, yes, I can feel tense while I’m technically “relaxing,” but usually there’s some specific responsibility hanging over me. It doesn’t feel totally random.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in situations where you might be observed or evaluated by others, like speaking in front of a group or even just eating in public?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, speaking in front of a group isn’t something I do a lot, so it’s hard for me to say how much of a pattern that is. I think most people would feel a little nervous if they had to present something, and I probably would too, but I don’t know that it’s a big defining thing for me.\n\nEating in public… no, not really. I don’t usually think about people watching me eat or judging how I’m acting in that kind of setting. If I’m at a restaurant or coffee shop, I’m usually just focused on whoever I’m with or what I’m doing.\n\nI guess if I felt like I was being evaluated, like someone was looking at my work or judging whether I seemed professional, that would feel different. But just being observed in normal public situations doesn’t really stand out as something I worry about much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about being judged or performing poorly in front of others affects areas of your life beyond work, such as attending events, participating in group activities, or engaging in everyday situations where you might be observed, even casually?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. If I feel like someone is assessing whether I’m professional or whether my work is good enough, then I can get really in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve avoided things in public for that reason, no. Or at least nothing that feels like a real pattern.

Like, I might choose not to speak up sometimes if I don’t feel strongly about something, or if I’m tired, but it’s not usually because I’m terrified people will judge me or I’ll embarrass myself. If I’m with friends or in a group and people are talking, I can give an opinion. I might overthink it a little afterward, like, “Was that awkward?” but I think that’s pretty normal and it doesn’t stop me from participating.

Being the center of attention isn’t my favorite thing, exactly. I’m not someone who loves all eyes on me. But I don’t think I’m avoiding normal non-work situations because of that. It’s more like I’d rather not make a big speech at a party, but if someone asked me a question or wanted my input, I could answer.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I’m regularly avoiding public things or social situations because I’m afraid of being judged or embarrassed. It doesn’t really show up that way for me.

And everyday things where people could casually observe me — being in a store, sitting in a café, walking around — I don’t usually feel especially self-conscious about that.\n\nIt feels different when it’s connected to work or seeming competent, if that makes sense. If I feel like someone is assessing whether I’m professional or whether my work is good enough, then I can get really in my head. But outside of that, I don’t think anxiety about being judged is spreading into everything. I’m not avoiding events or group activities because I’m worried people are watching me perform badly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided doing something in public—like speaking up, giving an opinion, or even just being the center of attention—because you were afraid of being judged or embarrassed, even in non-work situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided things in public for that reason, no. Or at least nothing that feels like a real pattern.\n\nLike, I might choose not to speak up sometimes if I don’t feel strongly about something, or if I’m tired, but it’s not usually because I’m terrified people will judge me or I’ll embarrass myself. If I’m with friends or in a group and people are talking, I can give an opinion. I might overthink it a little afterward, like, “Was that awkward?” but I think that’s pretty normal and it doesn’t stop me from participating.\n\nBeing the center of attention isn’t my favorite thing, exactly. I’m not someone who loves all eyes on me. But I don’t think I’m avoiding normal non-work situations because of that. It’s more like I’d rather not make a big speech at a party, but if someone asked me a question or wanted my input, I could answer.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’m regularly avoiding public things or social situations because I’m afraid of being judged or embarrassed. It doesn’t really show up that way for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of examples, because the ones you gave are kind of specific. Like, giving a toast at a party… I don’t know, I don’t think that comes up for me very often. If I knew I had to do something like that, I’d probably feel a little nervous beforehand, but more in the way that I think most people would — like wanting to say the right thing and not ramble or make it awkward. I don’t know that I’d be dreading it for days or trying to get out of it.

Being the new person in a group, I might feel a little unsure at first, just because you don’t know the group dynamic yet or how people relate to each other. I can be kind of in my head for a bit, like, “Am I talking too much?” or “Do I seem weird?” But I don’t think I usually feel like people are closely watching me or evaluating me in a harsh way. It’s more just adjusting to the situation.

So I guess… maybe some anticipatory nerves in situations where there’s attention on me, but I wouldn’t say it’s a major anxiety or something I’m constantly avoiding. It doesn’t feel like a big pattern outside of situations where I feel like my competence is actually being assessed.

It doesn’t really show up that way for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious ahead of time about situations where you think others might closely watch or evaluate you, like giving a toast at a party or being the new person in a group?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of examples, because the ones you gave are kind of specific.
